Multilinear matrix weights Thanks: Z. N. is supported by the Basque government through project GV IT1615-22
Spyridon Kakaroumpas, Zoe Nieraeth

Abstract
. In this work we fully characterize the classes of matrix weights for which multilinear Calderón–Zygmund operators extend to bounded operators on matrix weighted Lebesgue spaces. To this end, we develop the theory of multilinear singular integrals taking values in tensor products of finite dimensional Hilbert spaces. On the one hand, we establish quantitative bounds in terms of multilinear Muckenhoupt matrix weight characteristics and scalar Fujii–Wilson conditions of a tensor product analogue of the convex body sparse operator, of a convex-set valued tensor product analogue of the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator, and of a multilinear analogue of the Christ–Goldberg maximal operator. These bounds recover the sharpest known bounds in the linear case. Moreover, we define a notion of directional nondegeneracy for multilinear Calderón–Zygmund operators, which is new even in the scalar case. The noncommutavity of matrix multiplication, the absence of duality, and the natural presence of quasinorms in the multilinear setting present several new difficulties in comparison to previous works in the scalar or in the linear case. To overcome them, we use techniques inspired from convex combinatorics and differential geometry.



MnLargeSymbols’164
MnLargeSymbols’171

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 42B20, 46E25; Secondary: 46E30

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The Hunt–Muckenhoupt–Wheeden theorem

In the study of singular integrals, the problem of bounding Calderón–Zygmund operators on spaces of functions such as the weighted Lebesgue space

\|f\|_{L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}:=\Big(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\!|w(x)f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

is of fundamental interest. In
[
Muc72
]
, Muckenhoupt showed that the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator

Mf(x):=\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|f(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\ind_{Q}(x),

where the supremum is taken over all cubes
Q
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
is bounded in
L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
for
1<p\leq\infty
if and only if
w
satisfies the Muckenhoupt
A_{p}
condition
w\in A_{p}
:

[w]_{p}:=\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!w(x)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!w(x)^{-p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}<\infty.

Here
\tfrac{1}{p^{\prime}}:=1-\frac{1}{p}
, and the average is interpreted as an essential supremum when the exponent is infinite. This result was extended by Hunt, Muckenhoupt, and Wheeden in
[
HMW73
]
, who showed that all Calderón–Zygmund operators
T
are bounded in
L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
if and only if
1<p<\infty
and
w\in A_{p}
.

In the ‘90s, a certain vector-valued extension of the Hunt–Muckenhoupt–Wheeden theorem was sought after by Nazarov, Treil, and Volberg, see
[
Tre89
,
NT96
,
TV97a
,
TV97b
,
Vol97
]
. If
f:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n}
, a matrix weighted analogue of this problem can be formulated as follows: suppose
W:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n\times n}
is a.e. a Hermitian positive definite matrix and set

\|f\|_{L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})}:=\Big(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\!|W(x)f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Then one can ask when the pointwise extension
\widetilde{T}
of a Calderón–Zygmund operator
T
, defined by

(1.1)

\widetilde{T}f(x):=(Tf_{1}(x),\ldots,Tf_{n}(x)),

where
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
with
f_{k}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}
, is bounded in
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})
. Nazarov, Treil, and Volberg showed that this is the case precisely when
1<p<\infty
, and
W
satisfies the matrix Muckenhoupt
A_{p}
condition
W\in A_{p}
:
W^{-1}\in L^{p^{\prime}}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n\times n})
, and there is a
C\geq 1
such that for all cubes
Q\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{d}
and all
u\in\mathbf{C}^{n}
there is a
v\in\mathbf{C}^{n}
such that

\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|W(x)u|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|W(x)^{-1}v|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\leq C|\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{C}^{n}}|,

where
\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{C}^{n}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{k}\overline{v_{k}}
. We denote the smallest possible
C
by
[W]_{p}
. This is equivalent to the uniform boundedness in
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})
of the family of averaging operators

T_{Q}f:=\ind_{Q}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.18884ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

indexed over all cubes
Q
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, in which case we have

[W]_{p}=\sup_{Q}\|T_{Q}\|_{L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};C^{n})\to L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};C^{n})}.

To obtain a characterization of
A_{p}
in terms of a Hardy–Littlewood type maximal operator, in
[
CG01
]
, Christ and Goldberg defined the operator

M_{W}f(x):=\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|W(x)W(y)^{-1}f(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)\ind_{Q}(x),

and showed that
W\in A_{p}
precisely when
M_{W}:L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})\to L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. In order to prove the Rubio de Francia extrapolation theorem for matrix weights, Bownik and Cruz-Uribe in
[
BC23
]
needed an intrinsically defined maximal operator that maps a space to itself. To this end, they considered convex-set valued measurable mappings
F:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, where
\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
denotes the collection of compact symmetric convex sets in
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. They then defined
M^{\mathcal{K}}F(x)
to be the smallest set in
\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
containing the set

\bigcup_{Q}\,\langle F\rangle_{Q}\ind_{Q}(x),

where the average is defined through the so-called Aumann integral

\langle F\rangle_{Q}:=\Big\{\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!f\,\mathrm{d}x:f\in L^{0}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{R}^{n}),\,f(x)\in F(x)\,a.e.\Big\}.

This is well-defined, assuming
F
is
locally integrably bounded
, i.e., for each cube
Q
there is a constant
C
such that for all measurable
f:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}
with
f(x)\in F(x)
a.e. we have

\|f\|_{L^{1}(Q)}\leq C.

Defining
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n}))
as the measurable mappings
F:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
for which

h(x):=\sup_{u\in F(x)}|W(x)u|

satisfies
h\in L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, they showed that
W\in A_{p}
precisely when
1<p\leq\infty
and

M^{\mathcal{K}}:L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n}))\to L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}^{n})).

An alternative characterization of the matrix Muckenhoupt condition was found by Roudenko
[
Rou03
]
, who showed that

(1.2)

[W]_{p}\eqsim\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!\|W(x)W(y)^{-1}\|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
\|A\|
denotes the operator norm of a matrix
A\in\mathbf{C}^{n\times n}
. An essential ingredient in establishing this equivalence is the John ellipsoid theorem, which says that if
\rho:\mathbf{C}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
is a norm, then there is a Hermitian positive definite matrix
A\in\mathbf{C}^{n\times n}
for which

\rho(u)\leq|Au|\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho(u).

One can then apply this result to the norms

u\mapsto\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|W(x)u|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad u\mapsto\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|W(x)^{-1}u|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

to obtain the so-called reducing matrices
A_{W,Q,p}
,
A_{W^{-1},Q,p^{\prime}}
, which yield another characterization of
A_{p}
through

[W]_{p}\eqsim\sup_{Q}\|A_{W,Q,p}A_{W^{-1},Q,p^{\prime}}\|.

From this, one can deduce (
1.2
).

A different way of extending the Hunt–Muckenhoupt–Wheeden theorem is by considered
multilinear
Calderón–Zygmund operators
T
, which are singular integrals acting on
m
functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
. It was shown by Lerner, Ombrosi, Pérez, Torres, and Trujillo-González in
[
LOP+09
]
that such maps satisfy

T:L^{p}_{w_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\times\cdots L^{p}_{w_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),

where

w:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}w_{j},\quad\frac{1}{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}},

when
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
,
p<\infty
, and
\vec{w}\in A_{\vec{p}}
:

[\vec{w}]_{\vec{p}}:=\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!w(x)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!w_{j}(x)^{-p_{j}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{\prime}}}<\infty.

Moreover, they showed that the multilinear Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator

M(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})(x):=\sup_{Q}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|f_{j}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\ind_{Q}(x)

satisfies

M:L^{p}_{w_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\times\cdots\times L^{p}_{w_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

precisely when
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
and
w\in A_{\vec{p}}
.

1.2.
Main result

The goal of this paper is to fully establish a multilinear matrix weighted analogue of the Hunt–Muckenhoupt–Wheeden theorem, unifying and extending the theory for both the multilinear and matrix weighted settings.

First, we need to establish what we mean by extending a multilinear operator
T
to
m
-tuples of vector-valued functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
with
f_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
. To exemplify our approach, we first restrict to the case
m=2
,
n_{1}=n_{2}=2
. Given two functions
f,g:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{2}
, each have component functions
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
and
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
. As
T
is bilinear, we want our extension to consider all possible pairings of the component functions:

T(f_{1},g_{1}),\quad T(f_{1},g_{2}),\quad T(f_{2},g_{1}),\quad T(f_{2},g_{2}).

Arranging these functions in a matrix yields a mapping
\widetilde{T}(f,g):\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{2\times 2}\cong\mathbf{C}^{4}
.

In the general case, this extension can be conveniently expressed through the use of the tensor product

\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\cong\mathbf{C}^{n},\quad n:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}.

Suppose
T
is an
m
-linear operator in
L^{0}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
defined on
m
-tuples of functions in
L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Given vector-valued functions
\vec{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
with
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}})
, we define

\widetilde{T}(\vec{f}):\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}

by applying
T
“component-wise” to
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
. More precisely, denote the canonical basis of
\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
by
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{n_{j}}
so that each
f_{j}
can be written as

f_{j}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}}f_{j,k}(x)e_{k}.

Then our definition is

(1.3)

\widetilde{T}(\vec{f})(x):=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\cdots\sum_{k_{m}=1}^{n_{m}}T(f_{1,k_{1}},\ldots,f_{m,k_{m}})(x)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}e_{k_{j}}.

A standard check shows that this definition does not depend on the choice of orthonormal basis. As a matter of fact, in this work we will replace the
\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
by general
n_{j}
-dimensional Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, in which we work with this same extensions, even when no orthonormal basis is specified.

When
T
is the product map
T(h_{1},\ldots,h_{m})(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}h_{j}(x)
, our extension coincides with the tensor product map

\widetilde{T}(\vec{f})(x)=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\cdots\sum_{k_{m}=1}^{n_{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}f_{j,k_{j}}(x)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}e_{k_{j}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(x).

Moreover, when
m=1
, our extension reduces back to the component-wise extension (
1.1
).

We emphasize here that the proposed extension
\widetilde{T}
of
T
is the natural one obtained from the universal property of the tensor product.

Next, we need to establish what the appropriate multilinear Muckenhoupt condition is for matrix weights. Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
and let
W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}}
be matrix weights for which
W_{j}^{-1}\in L^{p_{j}^{\prime}}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}})
for
j=1,\ldots,m
. We set

\mathbf{W}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j},\quad\frac{1}{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}},

where for a.e.
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the map
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j}(x)
is the unique map satisfying the property that

\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j}(x)\Big)\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j}(x)u_{j}

for all
u_{j}\in\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
. Writing
\vec{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{m})
and
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
, we say that
\vec{W}
satisfies the
\vec{p}
matrix Muckenhoupt condition
\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
if for all cubes
Q
the mapping

T_{Q}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}):=\ind_{Q}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.18884ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!f_{j}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

satisfies

T_{Q}:L^{p_{1}}_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{1}})\times\cdots\times L^{p_{m}}_{W_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{m}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}\Big(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\Big),

with

[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}:=\sup_{Q}\|T_{Q}\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{1}})\times\cdots\times L^{p_{m}}_{W_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{m}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}\Big(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\Big)}<\infty.

Just like in the scalar case, extra difficulties arise in the multilinear setting due to the appearance of the exponents
p
in the quasi-Banach range
\tfrac{1}{m}<p<1
. In this work, we develop a way to apply the John ellipsoid theorem to the now quasinorms

\rho_{W,Q,p}(\mathbf{u}):=\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|\mathbf{W}(x)\mathbf{u}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

on
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
. To facilitate this, we make use of Caratheodory’s theorem on convex hulls. This result states that if
S
is a set of points in
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, then its convex hull consists precisely of points of the form

\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\theta_{k}u_{k},

where
0\leq\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
,
\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\theta_{k}=1
, and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n+1}\in S
. In Section
2.4
below we show how this allows us to obtain a reducing matrix
A_{\mathbf{W},Q,p}:\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\to\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
for which

K_{p}^{-2n}\rho_{\mathbf{W},Q,p}(\mathbf{u})\leq|A_{\mathbf{W},Q,p}\mathbf{u}|\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho_{\mathbf{W},Q,p}(\mathbf{u})

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
, where
K_{p}=2^{(\frac{1}{p}-1)_{+}}
is the quasi-metric constant of the Lebesgue space of exponent
p
. Using this allows us to show that our multilinear matrix
A_{\vec{p}}
condition can be characterized through the Roudenko-type condition

[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}\eqsim\sup_{Q}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!\|W_{j}(x)W_{j}(y_{j})^{-1}\|^{p_{j}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{j}\Big)^{\frac{p}{p_{j}^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},

see Proposition
4.3
below, as well as through the reducing matrix condition

[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}\eqsim\sup_{Q}\Big\|A_{W,Q,p}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{W_{j}^{-1},Q,p_{j}^{\prime}}\Big)\Big\|,

see Proposition
3.1
below. The proof of Proposition
3.1
itself is achieved through an involved induction scheme relying on some elementary tensor algebra. While such techniques are standard in the field of differential geometry, they have not appeared so far in the field of weighted estimates for singular integrals. Thus, we explain them in detail.

We also consider a characterization in terms of the tensor product maximal operator. For locally integrably bounded mappings
F_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, we define
M^{\mathcal{K}}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{m})(x)
as the smallest set in
\mathcal{K}\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\big)
containing

\bigcup_{Q}\,\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle F_{j}\rangle_{Q}\ind_{Q}(x).

Setting

L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}}):=L^{p_{1}}_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{1}})\times\cdots\times L^{p_{m}}_{W_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{m}}),

our main theorem is as follows:

Theorem A
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
,
\tfrac{1}{p}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tfrac{1}{p_{j}}>0
and let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{m}
be matrix weights
W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}}
with
W_{j}^{-1}\in L^{p_{j}^{\prime}}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}})
for
j=1,\ldots,m
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\widetilde{T}:L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}\Big(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\Big)
for all
m
-linear Calderón–Zygmund operators
T
;

(2)

M^{\mathcal{K}}:L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}\Big(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\Big)
;

(3)

\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
.

In order to establish this equivalence, we prove a general convex body domination principle for
m
-linear Calderón–Zygmund operators with kernels having a modulus of continuity satisfying the Dini condition, the precise definition of which we give in Section
6
. We defer the proof of Theorem
A
to Section
7
.

1.3.
Quantitative bounds

Following the Hunt–Muckenhoupt–Wheeden theorem, a natural question is how exactly the bounds of Calderón–Zygmund operators and the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator in
L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
depend on the Muckenhoupt characteristic
[w]_{p}
.

The sharp bound for the Hardy-Littlewood maximal operator was found by Buckley in
[
Buc93
]
, who showed that

\|M\|_{L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,p}[w]_{p}^{p^{\prime}}\quad\text{for $1<p\leq\infty$.}

As for matrix weights
W
, it was shown in
[
IM19
]
that the Christ–Goldberg maximal operator
M_{W}
satisfies its bound with the same exponent
p^{\prime}
:

\|M_{W}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})\to L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,n,p}[W]_{p}^{p^{\prime}}.

Reducing back to this result, it was shown in
[
BC23
]
that the convex-set valued maximal operator
M^{\mathcal{K}}
also satisfies

\|M^{\mathcal{K}}\|_{L_{W}^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})\to L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})}\lesssim_{d,n,p}[W]_{p}^{p^{\prime}}.

The problem for Calderón–Zygmund operators
T
was a much harder problem, and became known as the
A_{2}
conjecture. It was eventually settled by Hytönen in
[
Hyt12
]
, who proved the validity of the conjectured bound

(1.4)

\|T\|_{L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,p,T}[w]_{p}^{\max\{p,p^{\prime}\}}.

The proof was simplified by Lerner in
[
Ler13
]
, who showed that for each Calderón-Zygmund operator
T
there is a constant
C_{T}
such that for all
f\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
there is a
\tfrac{1}{2\cdot 3^{d}}
-sparse
collection of cubes
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that

(1.5)

|Tf(x)|\leq C_{T}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{S}}\ind_{Q}(x)\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.18884ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!|f(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x=:C_{T}A_{\mathcal{S}}f

for a.e.
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
. For
0<\eta<1
, a collection of cubes
\mathcal{S}
is called
\eta
-sparse if it satisfies the packing condition

\Big|\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{S}\\
Q\subsetneq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}Q\Big|\leq(1-\eta)|Q_{0}|

for all
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
. This reduced the proof of (
1.4
) to proving a straightforward of
A_{\mathcal{S}}f
, uniform in the sparse collection
\mathcal{S}
.

This technique was not readily adaptable to the case of matrix weights. Indeed, unlike in the scalar case, a pointwise bound like (
1.5
) does not guarantee a bound of
|W(x)\widetilde{T}f(x)|
for matrix weights
W
. This issue was solved by Nazarov, Petermichl, Treil, and Volberg in the seminal work
[
NPTV17
]
. They introduced the notion of
convex body domination
, where they showed the existence of a constant
C_{T}>0
such that for each
f\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})
there exists a sparse collection
\mathcal{S}
for which

\widetilde{T}f(x)\in C_{T}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{S}}\llangle f\rrangle_{Q}\ind_{Q}(x)=:C_{T}A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}f(x).

Here the sum is treated as a Minkowski sum, scalar multiples are considered pointwise, and the double angle bracket notation denotes the Aumann integral

\llangle f\rrangle_{Q}:=\langle\mathcal{K}(f)\rangle_{Q}=\Big\{\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!h(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:h\in L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),\,\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\leq 1\Big\},

where
\mathcal{K}(f)(x)
denotes the smallest set in
\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{C}^{n})
containing
f(x)
. Unlike what happened for the maximal operator, this does not recover the scalar bound (
1.4
). Instead, for the case
p=2
, they proved that

\|\widetilde{T}\|_{L^{2}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{2}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,n}C_{T}[W]_{2}^{3}.

Rather surprisingly, this bound was shown to be sharp for the Hilbert transform
T=H
by Domelevo, Petermichl, Treil, and Volberg in
[
DPTV24
]
. For general exponents, it was shown by Cruz-Uribe, Isralowitz, and Moen in
[
CIM18
]
that

(1.6)

\|\widetilde{T}\|_{L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,n,p}C_{T}[W]_{p}^{p+p^{\prime}-1}\quad\text{if $1<p<\infty$.}

In bounding the convex body operator
A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}
in
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{C}^{n}))
, a main ingredient is the sharp reverse Hölder inequality of
[
HP13
]
for scalar weights
v
satisfying the Fujii–Wilson condition

[v]_{\text{FW}}:=\sup_{Q}\frac{1}{v(Q)}\int_{Q}\!M^{\mathcal{D}(Q)}(v)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

where
M^{\mathcal{D}(Q)}
is the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator taken over the dyadic subgrid of the cube
Q
. By restricting the averaging operators
T_{Q}
to one-dimensional subspaces of
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{n})
, one finds that if
W\in A_{p}
, then also
|Wu|\in A_{p}
for all
u\in\mathbf{C}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, with

\sup_{u\in\mathbf{C}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}[|Wu|]_{p}\leq[W]_{p}.

Thus, as
W\in A_{p}
precisely if
W^{-1}\in A_{p^{\prime}}
, the Fujii–Wilson conditions

[W]_{\text{FW}_{p}}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbf{C}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}[|Wu|^{p}]^{\frac{1}{p}}_{\text{FW}}\lesssim_{d}[W]_{p},\quad[W^{-1}]_{\text{FW}_{p^{\prime}}}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbf{C}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}[|Wu|^{p^{\prime}}]^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}_{\text{FW}}\lesssim_{d}[W]_{p}

hold. This yields a uniform reverse Hölder inequality which is used in the proof of (
1.6
) to deduce that

\|\widetilde{T}\|_{L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,n,p}C_{T}[W]^{\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}}_{\text{FW}_{p}}[W^{-1}]^{\frac{p^{\prime}}{p}}_{\text{FW}_{p^{\prime}}}[W]_{p}\quad\text{if $1<p<\infty$.}

However, in the multilinear case, these uniform reverse Hölder conditions are not readily available. Indeed, while the restriction technique to one dimensional subspaces still works, this does not yield the required scalar bounds (see Remark
4.6
below). Moreover, the notion of duality yielding
W^{-1}\in A_{p^{\prime}}
for
W\in A_{p}
is now completely absent.

In the scalar case, the multilinear Muckenhoupt condition is equivalent to a set of individual matrix linear Muckenhoupt conditions on each of the weights
w_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
and
w
, see
[
LOP+09
,
LMO20
,
Nie20
]
. It turns out that a similar result continues being true in the matrix setting. However, the noncommutative nature of matrix multiplication imposes additional technical difficulties, which we are able to overcome through a careful use of Hölder’s inequality. We refer to Theorem
4.10
below for details.

Reverse Höler inequalities in the scalar case follow immediately from the individual Muckenhoupt conditions. In the matrix case the individual matrix Muckenhoupt conditions one obtains are intrisically of a “two-exponent nature” but at the same time different from the ones that have appeared in other places in the literature such as
[
IM19
,
KNV24
]
. In order to deduce scalar Muckenhoupt conditions from them we need to test a new type of “exponent-shifted” averaging operators on one dimensional subspaces, see Proposition
4.11
below. Using this, we find that if
\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
, then

[\mathbf{W}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p}}:=\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\Big)\setminus\{0\}}[|\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}|^{p}]_{\text{FW}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},p}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}\quad\text{ if }p<\infty,

and

[W_{j}^{-1}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p_{j}^{\prime}}}:=\sup_{u_{j}\in\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\setminus\{0\}}[|W_{j}^{-1}u_{j}|^{p_{j}^{\prime}}]_{\mathrm{FW}}^{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{\prime}}}\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},p_{j}}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}\quad\text{if $p_{j}>1$ for }j=1,\ldots,m,

see Corollary
4.13
coupled with Proposition
4.3
below. Defining

A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\vec{F})(x):=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{K}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle F_{j}\rangle_{Q}\Big)\ind_{Q}(x),

where for
S\subseteq\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}
we let
\mathcal{K}(S)
denote the smallest set in
\mathcal{K}\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}}\big)
containing
S
, our main quantitative result is:

Theorem B
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
,
\tfrac{1}{p}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tfrac{1}{p_{j}}
, let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{m}
be matrix weights
W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}}
, and let
0<\eta<1
. Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

If
p<\infty
and
\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
, then uniformly for all
\eta
-sparse collections
\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle\|A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}\|_{L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},\vec{p},\eta}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}[\mathbf{W}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p}}^{(p-1)^{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}[W_{j}^{-1}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p_{j}^{\prime}}}^{\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},p}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}^{\max(1,p)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}};

(2)

If
\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\|M^{\mathcal{K}}\|_{L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},\vec{p}}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}[W_{j}^{-1}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p_{j}^{\prime}}}^{\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n}}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}^{1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}}.

Part
1
is proven as Theorem
6.10
below, and part
2
as Theorem
5.3
below.

Interestingly, when
m=1
, the bound in
2
recovers Buckley’s sharp bound of the scalar result. However, in the multilinear case, the sharp bound for
M
was shown in
[
LMS14
]
(see
[
Nie19
]
for the case of infinite exponents) to be

\|M\|_{L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{w}}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to L^{p}_{w}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{p}}[\vec{w}]_{\vec{p}}^{\max\{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}\}}.

This exponent is smaller than the one we obtain in
2
. We leave it as an open problem whether the bound in the matrix case can be improved or not.

We apply Theorem
B
to prove implication
3
\Rightarrow
1
of Theorem
A
with the following convex body domination result. Here, we write
A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\vec{f}):=A_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{K}(f_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{K}(f_{m}))
.

Theorem C
.

Let
T
be an
m
-linear Calderón–Zygmund operator with a kernel having a modulus of continuity that satisfies the Dini condition. Then there is a constant
C_{T}>0
(depending on
d
,
m
,
\vec{n}
, and
T
) such that for any cube
Q_{0}
and any
m
-tuple of functions
\vec{f}\in L^{\vec{1}}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})
there exists a
\tfrac{1}{2\cdot 3^{d}}
-sparse family
\mathcal{S}
of cubes in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
contained in
3Q_{0}
such that

T(\vec{f})(x)\in C_{T}A^{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\vec{f})(x)\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in Q_{0},

where the constant
C_{T}
depends only on
d
,
m
,
\vec{n}
and
T
.

In particular, if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty]
satisfy
\tfrac{1}{p}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tfrac{1}{p_{j}}>0
, and
W_{1},\ldots,W_{m}
are matrix weights
W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}}
with
\vec{W}\in A_{\vec{p}}
, then

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{T}\|_{L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C}^{\vec{n}})\to L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{C}^{n_{j}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},\vec{p}}C_{T}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}[\mathbf{W}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p}}^{(p-1)^{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}[W_{j}^{-1}]_{\mathrm{FW}_{p_{j}^{\prime}}}^{\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,m,\vec{n},\vec{p}}C_{T}[\vec{W}]_{\vec{p}}^{\max(1,p)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{p_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}}.

This convex body domination result is proven in Theorem
6.9
below, and the quantitative bounds in Corollary
6.11
below.

While this result is certainly known, we note that even in the scalar case, sparse domination for
m
-linear Calderón-Zygmund operators with kernels having a modulus of continuity that satisfies the Dini condition has not been explicitly written in the literature. In
[
CR16
,
LN18
]
, the less general
\log
-Dini condition is assumed, and in
[
DHL18
]
, only the case
m=2
is treated. For a complete exposition, we provide details of the general case
m>1
in this work based on
[
DHL18
,
Li18
]
.

1.4.
Organization

This paper is organized as follows:

•

In Section
2
we lay the groundwork for the techniques and results of this paper. On the one hand, we introduce our main notation and collect together some notions and facts on directional quasi-Banach functions spaces, tensor products of Hilbert spaces, equivalent norms on spaces of linear operators and sparse families. Of particular importance is a novel definition of a product of directional Banach functions spaces, each of which is obtained from a Banach function space through a matrix weight. On the other hand, inspired by techniques in convex combinatorics we introduce a completely new notion of reducing operators associated to quasinorms, which forms one of the cornerstones of the paper.

•

In Section
3
we characterize the boundedness of averaging operators from a cartesian product of directional Banach function spaces into a directional quasi-Banach function space in terms of reducing operators. The new reducing operators for quasinorms are the centerpiece of this characterization. On the technical level, inspired by techniques in differential geometry we use an involved but at the same time rather elementary induction scheme involving some tensor algebra.

•

In Section
4
we develop a theory of multilinear Muckenhoupt characteristics for matrix weights. Thanks to the new notion of reducing operators for quasinorms, we are able to do so directly in the full generality of three sets of parameters and fully recover the results that were already known in the scalar multilinear case. The main result of this section is a set of reverse Hölder estimates for each weight individually as well as their tensor product. One of the main tools of the proof is the use of novel “exponent-shifted” averaging operators.

•

In Section
5
we treat lower and upper bounds for a tensor product analog of the convex body maximal operator. First, we show the equivalence of matrix weighted weak-type estimates for this operator and the multilinear Muckenhoupt condition. Then, we show that in the presence of the multilinear Muckenhoupt condition, the tensor product convex body maximal operator admits matrix weighted, strong type bounds. The proof relies on the reverse Hölder estimates of Section
4
. Finally, we prove a pointwise sparse domintation result for the tensor product convex body maximal operator.

•

In Section
6
we consider matrix weighted bounds for multilinear Calderón–Zygmund operators. After a quick overview of basic facts about such operators we prove a pointwise sparse domination result for them in terms of a tensor product convex body sparse operator. This is achieved through bootstrapping the sparse domination algorithm of the scalar case. Using our sparse domination and the reverse Hölder estimates from Section
4
, we prove matrix weighted upper bounds for them in the presence of the multilinear Muckenhoupt condition. Finally, we introduce a novel notion of directionally nondegenerate multilinear Calderón–Zygmund operators – which is new even in the scalar case – and show that matrix weighted bounds for them imply the multilinear Muckenhoupt condition. As an illustration of our notion of directional nondegeneracy, we study the concrete example of multilinear Riesz transforms.

•

In Section
7
we prove theorem
A
by collecting together all pieces from the preceding sections.

•

Finally, for the convenience of the reader, in the Appendix we give detailed proofs of several facts about multilinear Calderón–Zygmund operators used in the sparse domination of Section
6
.

2.
preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

Throughout this work, we let
d
be a positive integer denoting the dimension of the domain, and let
m
be a positive integer denoting the multilinearity. We let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{m})
be finite dimensional Hilbert spaces that are all defined over the field
\mathbf{F}
, where
\mathbf{F}
denotes either
\mathbf{R}
or
\mathbf{C}
. We fix their dimensions
n_{j}:=\dim\mathcal{H}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
and set

n:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}.

Choosing an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, we may identify
\mathcal{H}_{j}\cong\mathbf{F}^{n_{j}}
.

We will use the following definitions and notational conventions:

•

The components of
\vec{p}
will always be denoted by
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
, and, per convention,

\frac{1}{p}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}}.

Algebraic operations and inequalities of
\vec{p}
are considered in the Schur sense, i.e., componentwise. For example, we write
\tfrac{1}{\vec{p}}-\tfrac{1}{\vec{q}}=(\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{1}{q_{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{p_{m}}-\frac{1}{q_{m}})
, or
\vec{p}>\vec{q}
if
p_{j}>q_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
.

•

As a convention, we write

\mathcal{H}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{j},

which is a Hilbert space with respect to the unique sesquilinear form on
\mathcal{H}
satisfying

\Big\langle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\langle u_{j},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}

for all
\vec{u},\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
. Note that
\dim\mathcal{H}=n
.

•

The space of bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
and is equipped with the operator norm

\|L\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}:=\sup_{\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 1}\|Lu\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

If
\dim\mathcal{V}=k\in\mathbf{N}
and an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{V}
is chosen, we may identify
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
with the space
\mathbf{F}^{k\times k}
of
k\times k
matrices.

•

An
m
-tuple of mappings
\vec{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{m})
,
W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
will be called an
m
-tuple of matrix weights if for a.e.
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
the operator
W_{j}(x)
is self-adjoint and positive for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. When identified with a matrix, this means that
W_{j}(x)
is Hermitian and positive definite. Moreover, as a convention, we set

\mathbf{W}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}).

•

For a finite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}
over
\mathbf{R}
, respectively over
\mathbf{C}
, we let
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
denote the non-empty, compact, real, respectively complex symmetric, convex subsets of
\mathcal{V}
. Given
0<p\leq\infty
and a matrix weight
W:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
, the space
L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
consists of the measurable mappings
F:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
for which
\|W(x)F(x)\|_{\mathcal{V}}:=\sup_{u\in F(x)}\|W(x)u\|_{\mathcal{V}}
lies in
L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, with

\|F\|_{L^{p}_{W}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))}:=\big\|\|WF\|_{\mathcal{V}}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}.

•

For an
m
-tuple of exponents
\vec{p}
and an
m
-tuple of matrix weights
\vec{W}
we write

L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\vec{\mathcal{H}}):=L^{p_{1}}_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ldots\times L^{p_{m}}_{W_{m}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{H}_{m}).

Thus, we write
\vec{f}\in L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\vec{\mathcal{H}})
to mean that
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
with
f_{j}\in L^{p_{j}}_{W_{j}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{H}_{j})
for
j=1,\ldots,m
. We analogously define
L^{\vec{p}}_{\vec{W}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\vec{\mathcal{H}}))
,
L^{0}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\vec{\mathcal{H}})
,
L^{\vec{\infty}}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\vec{\mathcal{H}})
, …

•

By a cube
Q\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we mean a cube whose sides are parallel to the coordinate axes.

We write
A\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
to mean that there is a constant
C_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,}
depending on the parameters
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
for which
A\leq C_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
. We define
A\gtrsim_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
analogously, and we write
A\eqsim_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
if both
A\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
and
A\gtrsim_{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots}B
hold.

2.2.
Directional quasi-Banach function spaces

In this section we describe the directional quasi-Banach function spaces as defined in
[
Nie24a
]
. Let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space and let
\mathcal{V}
be a finite dimensional Hilbert space. We say that
\mathbf{X}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
if it is a complete quasi-normed subspace of
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
and satisfies the following properties:

•

The directional ideal property:
For all
f\in\mathbf{X}
and
g\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
satisfying
g(x)\in\mathcal{K}(f)(x)
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
, we have
g\in\mathbf{X}
with
\|g\|_{\mathbf{X}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}
;

•

Non-degeneracy:
If
g\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
satisfies
\int_{\Omega}\!\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0
for all
f\in\mathbf{X}
, then
g=0
.

We let
K_{\mathbf{X}}\geq 1
denote the smallest constant for which

\|f+g\|_{\mathbf{X}}\leq K_{\mathbf{X}}(\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}+\|g\|_{\mathbf{X}})

for all
f,g\in\mathbf{X}
. When
K_{\mathbf{X}}=1
, we call
\mathbf{X}
a
\mathcal{V}
-directional Banach function space over
\Omega
.

We define
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
as those
g\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
for which

\|g\|_{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}}:=\sup_{\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}=1}\int_{\Omega}\!|\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty.

As in
[
Nie24a
, Proposition 3.2]
, we have
g\in\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
if and only if there is a
C\geq 0
such that for all
f\in\mathbf{X}
we have

(2.1)

\Big|\int_{\Omega}\!\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}.

In this case, the optimal
C
satisfies
\|g\|_{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}}=C
.

We say that
\mathbf{X}
satisfies the
Fatou property
, if for any sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\mathbf{X}
with
f_{k}\to f
a.e. in
\mathcal{V}
, and
\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathbf{X}}<\infty
, we have
f\in\mathbf{X}
with

\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathbf{X}}.

If
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{F}
, then we simply call a
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
a
quasi-Banach function space
over
\Omega
, and a Banach function space over
\Omega
if the triangle inequality is satisfied. We refer the reader to
[
LN24
]
for a thorough treatment of quasi-Banach function spaces.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space. For each
v\in\mathcal{V}
we define the space
\mathbf{X}_{v}
consisting of those
h\in L^{0}(\Omega)
for which
hv\in\mathbf{X}
, with
\|h\|_{\mathbf{X}_{v}}:=\|hv\|_{\mathbf{X}}
. We say that
\mathbf{X}
has the
component-wise saturation property
if for an orthonormal basis
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
of
\mathcal{V}
, the spaces
\mathbf{X}_{e_{k}}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
are quasi-Banach function spaces.

A measurable mapping
W:\Omega\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
is called a
matrix weight
if
W(x)
is self-adjoint and positive for a.e.
x\in\Omega
. Given a quasi-Banach function space
X
over
\Omega
and a matrix weight
W:\Omega\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
, we define
X_{W}
as the space of functions
f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
for which
\|Wf\|_{\mathcal{V}}\in X
, and set

\|f\|_{X_{W}}:=\big\|\|Wf\|_{\mathcal{V}}\big\|_{X}.

Then
X_{W}
is an
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
with the component-wise saturation property satisfying

(X_{W})^{\prime}=(X^{\prime})_{W^{-1}},

see
[
Nie24a
, Section 3.2]
. Observe that if
X
satisfies the Fatou property, then so does
X_{W}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\vec{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
be
m
Banach function spaces over
\Omega
. Their product
X:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}X_{j}
is defined as the quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
consisting of those
f\in L^{0}(\Omega)
for which there exist
0\leq f_{j}\in X_{j}
such that
|f|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}
, with

(2.2)

\|f\|_{X}:=\inf\Big\{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|f_{j}\|_{X_{j}}:|f|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}f_{j},\,0\leq f_{j}\in X_{j}\Big\}.

We refer to
[
Sch10
]
for a thorough treatment of the properties of such product spaces.

We would like to define the product of a finite number of directional Banach function spaces as well. Although we have not been able to do so in full generality, we give a definition in the particular case of matrix weighted Banach function spaces, including matrix weighted Lebesgue spaces.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\vec{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
be
m
Banach functions spaces over
\Omega
and let
\vec{W}
be a
m
-tuple of matrix weights. For each
j=1,\ldots,m
, consider the
\mathcal{H}_{j}
-directional Banach function space
\mathbf{X}_{j}:=(X_{j})_{W_{j}}
. The product
\mathbf{X}:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{X}_{j}
of
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{m}
is defined to be

(2.3)

\mathbf{X}=X_{\mathbf{W}},

where
X:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}X_{j}
and, we recall that
\mathbf{W}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}W_{j}
. Observe that
\mathbf{X}
thus defined is an
\mathcal{H}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
.

In the special case that there is
\vec{p}\in[1,\infty]^{m}
with
X_{j}=L^{p_{j}}(\Omega)
for each
j=1,\ldots,m
, we have
X=L^{p}(\Omega)
, where we recall that
\frac{1}{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}}
. Hence, in this case,

\prod_{j=1}^{m}L^{p_{j}}_{W_{j}}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{j})=L^{p}_{\mathbf{W}}(\Omega;\mathcal{H}).

2.3.
Linear operators on a tensor product of Hilbert spaces

Let
\vec{\mathcal{H}}
be a
m
-tuple of finite dimensional Hilbert spaces. We recall that
\mathcal{H}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{j}
is also a finite dimensional Hilbert space. Given now
A_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
for each
j=1,\ldots,m
, there is a unique operator
A
satisfying

A\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{j}u_{j}

for all
\vec{u}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
. We denote this operator by
\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{j}:=A
. In this case one can directly verify that

\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{j}\Big)\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}\Big)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}(A_{j}B_{j})

for all
B_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
.

Notice that

A^{*}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{j}^{*}.

In particular, if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
are Hermitian, then
A
is also Hermitian. Moreover, using for example the spectral theorem one readily deduces that if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
are all positive definite, then
A
is also positive definite.

The following lemma is a standard result, but we include its proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
A_{j}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
for each
j=1,\ldots,m
. Set
A:=\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{j}
. Then, we have

\|A\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|A_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{j}\to\mathcal{H}_{j}}.

Proof.

If all
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
are all positive semidefinite, then the claim follows immediately through an application of the spectral theorem.

For the general case, observe first that

A^{\ast}A=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}(A_{j}^{\ast}A_{j}).

It follows from the special case that

\displaystyle\|A\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\|A^{*}A\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|A_{j}^{*}A_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{j}\to\mathcal{H}_{j}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|A_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{j}\to\mathcal{H}_{j}}^{2},

concluding the proof.
∎

2.4.
Reducing operators for quasinorms

The construction of reducing operators in the case of norms is a standard application of the John ellipsoid theorem. However, since in the multilinear setting we are working with Lebesgue spaces with exponent
p<1
, we need to consider the setting of quasinorms as well.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space, and let
q:\mathcal{V}\to[0,\infty)
be a lower semicontinuous quasinorm with quasi-triangle inequality constant
K
. Then there is a self-adjoint positive
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
for which

K^{-2n}q(u)\leq\|Au\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}}q(u),

for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
.

When the underlying field is
\mathbf{R}
, this power
2n
can be replaced by
n
. The main ingredient for the proof other than the John ellipsoid theorem is Carathéodory’s theorem on convex hulls. It states that if
S\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{n}
is a set, then for any
u
belonging to the convex hull of
S
there are
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n+1}\geq 0
with
\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\theta_{k}=1
, and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n+1}\in S
for which

u=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\theta_{k}u_{k}.

The fact that the amount of elements in this convex sum has a fixed dimensional upper bound makes the result possible. When the underlying field is
\mathbf{C}
, we can use the canonical identification
\mathbf{C}\cong\mathbf{R}^{2}
to obtain this result with
n
replaced by
2n
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

We define

L:=\text{conv}(\{u\in\mathcal{V}:q(u)\leq 1\})\subseteq\mathcal{V}.

We claim that this is a compact (complex) symmetric convex set containing
0
in its interior. By Carathéodory’s theorem on convex hulls, the convex hull of a compact set is compact, so it suffices to show that the set of
u\in\mathcal{V}
with
q(u)\leq 1
has these properties. To this end, note that there are
c,C>0
for which

c\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq q(u)\leq C\|u\|_{\mathcal{V}}

for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
. Boundedness then follows from the first inequality, whereas the containment of ball centered at
0
follows from the second. For a topological argument proving the existence of these constants we refer the reader to
[
CM21
, Theorem 2.5]
. Finally, to see that the set is closed, let
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence for which
q(u_{k})\leq 1
and
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}
. Then, by lower semicontinuity of
q
, we have

q(u)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}q(u_{k})\leq 1,

as desired.

Now, we may apply the John ellipsoid theorem to
L
to find a self-adjoint positive definite mapping
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
satisfying

(2.4)

A^{-1}\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq L\subseteq n^{\frac{1}{2}}A^{-1}\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}.

By Carathéodory’s theorem on convex hulls, for each
u\in L
, there are
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1}\geq 0
with
\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\theta_{k}=1
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{2n+1}\in\mathcal{V}
with
q(u_{k})\leq 1
for
k=1,\ldots,n
such that
u=\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\theta_{k}u_{k}
. Hence, by inductively using the quasi-triangle inequality, we have

q(u)\leq K^{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\theta_{k}q(u_{k})\leq K^{2n}.

This proves that

L\subseteq\{u\in\mathcal{V}:q(u)\leq K^{2n}\}.

Hence, by (
2.4
), we find that for any
u\in\mathcal{V}
we have

K^{-2n}q(u)\leq\|Au\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}}q(u),

as desired.
∎

The following application of this result proves the existence of reducing operators in directional quasi-Banach function spaces.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space, let
\mathbf{X}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
with the Fatou property, and let
E\subseteq\Omega
satisfy
0<\mu(E)<\infty
. Suppose that
\mathbf{X}
has the property that
\ind_{E}u\in\mathbf{X}
for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
. Then there is a self-adjoint positive
A_{\mathbf{X},E}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
satisfying

(2.5)

K_{\mathbf{X}}^{-2n}\|\ind_{E}u\|_{\mathbf{X}}\leq\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\ind_{E}u\|_{\mathbf{X}}

for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
.

The operator
A_{\mathbf{X},E}
is called the
reducing operator
of
E
in
\mathbf{X}
.

Proof of Corollary
2.5
.

By Proposition
2.4
we need only check that the quasinorm
u\mapsto\|\ind_{E}u\|_{\mathbf{X}}
is lower semicontinuous. Indeed, if
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}
, then
\ind_{E}u_{k}\to\ind_{E}u
a.e. and thus, by the Fatou property of
\mathbf{X}
, we have

\|\ind_{E}u\|_{\mathbf{X}}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\ind_{E}u_{k}\|_{\mathbf{X}},

as desired.
∎

2.5.
Equivalent norms on the space of linear operators

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a
n
-dimensional Hilbert space for an integer
n\geq 1
, and let
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{V}
. Let
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
. We claim that

(2.6)

\|A\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}\eqsim_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

Indeed, we have

\|A\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}\geq\sup_{k=1,\ldots,n}\|Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

For the converse inequality, an application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality combined with the bound
\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{1}}
yields

\|Av\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\Big)\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}}

for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, proving the the claim.

In practice, (
2.6
) is used for the following result:

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
X
be a quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
with the Fatou property, let
\mathcal{V}
be a
n
-dimensional Hilbert space with
n\geq 1
an integer, and let
W:\Omega\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
be a matrix weight. Set
\mathbf{X}:=X_{W}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\ind_{E}\|WA\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}\|_{X}

\displaystyle\eqsim_{n,K_{X}}\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}A\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}},\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V}).

Proof.

Pick an orthonormal basis
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
for
\mathcal{V}
. Then, by (
2.5
) and (
2.6
),

\displaystyle\|\ind_{E}\|WA\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}\|_{X}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n,K_{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\ind_{E}\|WAe\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|_{X}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\ind_{E}Ae_{k}\|_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\eqsim_{n}\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}A\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}.

For the lower bound, note that, by (
2.5
) and (
2.6
),

\displaystyle\|\ind_{E}\|WA\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}\|_{X}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{k=1,\ldots,n}\|\ind_{E}\|WAe_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|_{X}=\sup_{k=1,\ldots,n}\|\ind_{E}Ae_{k}\|_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}Ae_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\eqsim_{n}\|A_{\mathbf{X},E}A\|_{\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}}.

The result follows.
∎

2.6.
Convex-set valued mappings

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space over
\mathbf{F}
. We let
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
denote the non-empty, closed subsets
K
of
\mathcal{V}
satisfying:

•

Convexity:
If
u,v\in K
, then
(1-t)u+tv\in K
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
;

•

Symmetry:
If
u\in K
, then
\lambda u\in K
for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}
with
|\lambda|=1
.

Given a set
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we define
\mathcal{K}(S)
as the smallest set in
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
containing
S
.

Let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. We call a mapping
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})

measurable
if for every open set
E\subseteq\mathcal{V}
the set

F^{-1}(E):=\{x\in\Omega:E\cap F(x)\neq\varnothing\}

is measurable. Moreover, we let
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(V))
denote the space of measurable mappings
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
.

Given a
F\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
, we define its set of
selections
by

S^{0}(\Omega;F):=\{f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{V}):f(x)\in F(x)\text{ a.e.}\}.

Given a
\mathcal{V}
-directional quasi-Banach function space
\mathbf{X}
, we let
\mathbf{X}[\mathcal{K}]
denote the set of those
F\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
for which
S^{0}(\Omega;F)
is a bounded subset of
\mathbf{X}
. Moreover, in this case we set

\|F\|_{\mathbf{X}[\mathcal{K}]}:=\sup_{f\in S^{0}(\Omega;F)}\|f\|_{\mathbf{X}}.

If
X
is a quasi-Banach function space with the Fatou property and
W:\Omega\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
is a matrix weight, then
F\in X_{W}[\mathcal{K}]
if and only if
F(x)
is a bounded set for a.e.
x\in\Omega
, and
h(x):=\sup_{u\in F(x)}|W(x)u|
satisfies
h\in X
. Moreover, writing
\|WF\|_{\mathcal{V}}:=h
, in this case we have

\|F\|_{X_{W}[\mathcal{K}]}=\big\|\|WF\|_{\mathcal{V}}\big\|_{X},

see
[
Nie24a
, Proposition 3.10 and 3.11]
. In particular, this is true for matrix weighted Lebesgue spaces, where we write

L^{p}_{W}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})):=L^{p}_{W}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})[\mathcal{K}].

Furthermore, when
W
is the identity mapping, we remove the subscript
W
.

If
F\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
, then we define the
Aumann integral
of
F
by

\int_{\Omega}\!F\,\mathrm{d}\mu:=\Big\{\int_{\Omega}\!f\,\mathrm{d}\mu:f\in S^{0}(\Omega;F)\Big\}.

Note that this is a well-defined set in
\mathcal{V}
, since any
f\in S^{0}(\Omega;F)
belongs to
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathcal{V})
. Denoting the supremum of the norms of a bounded set
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
by
\|S\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, we have the following result:

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
F\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
. Then the Aumann integral of
F
is a non-empty, convex, symmetric, and bounded set, with bound

\Big\|\int_{\Omega}\!F\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\eqsim_{n}\int_{\Omega}\!\|F\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

Proof.

For any
f\in S^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{F})
we have

\Big\|\int_{\Omega}\!f\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\int_{\Omega}\!\|f\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\|F\|_{L^{1}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))}=\int_{\Omega}\!\|F\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

Taking a supremum over all
f\in S^{0}(\Omega;F)
, this implies that

\Big\|\int_{\Omega}\!F\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\int_{\Omega}\!\|F\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

For the converse inequality, pick
f\in S^{0}(\Omega;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
for which
\|f\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\|F\|_{\mathcal{V}}
. Let
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
bean orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{V}
and set

f_{k}:=\frac{|\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}|}{\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}}f

on the support of
\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}
. As
F
is a symmetric set a.e., we have
f_{k}\in S^{0}(\Omega;F)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\!\|f\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\Omega}\!|\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big\langle\int_{\Omega}\!f_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,e_{k}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big\|\int_{\Omega}\!f_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq n\Big\|\int_{\Omega}\!F\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Big\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

The assertion follows.
∎

We say that
F\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
if
\ind_{Q}F\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V}))
for all cubes
Q\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{d}
. In this case the Aumann integral

\langle F\rangle_{Q}=\Big\{\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q}}\!f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:f\in S^{0}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\ind_{Q}F)\Big\}

is a well-defined bounded set in
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{V})
for all cubes
Q\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

For proofs and further properties of set-valued mappings, we refer the reader to
[
AF09
,
BC23
,
Nie24a
]
.

2.7.
Sparse families

In this subsection we briefly describe the definitions and relevant properties of sparse families that we need in this work. Further details and proofs can be found in, e.g.,
[
LN18
]
and
[
NPTV17
]
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
0<\eta<1
. A family of cubes
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
is said to be
\eta
-sparse, if there exists a family
\{E_{Q}:\penalty\ Q\in\mathcal{S}\}
of pairwise disjoint measurable subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
with
E_{Q}\subseteq Q
and
|E_{Q}|\geq\eta|Q|
for all
Q\in\mathcal{S}
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a dyadic grid in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. A subfamily
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{D}
is said to be
martingale
\varepsilon
-sparse
if for all
Q\in\mathcal{S}
we have

\sum_{R\in\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)}|R|\leq\varepsilon|Q|,

where
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
is the family of all maximal cubes in
\mathcal{S}
that are strictly contained in
Q
.

Setting
E_{Q}=Q\backslash\bigcup_{R\in\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)}R
, we find that a martingale
\varepsilon
-sparse collection is
1-\varepsilon
-sparse.

We will often use the so-called “
3^{d}
-lattice trick” (see
[
LN18
, Theorem 3.1]
), stating that there are
3^{d}
dyadic grids
(\mathcal{D}^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{3^{d}}
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, such that for each cube
Q\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{d}
there are
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,3^{d}\}
and
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\alpha}
with
Q\subseteq R
and
|R|\leq 6^{d}|Q|
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{S}
is an
\eta
-sparse family for some
0<\eta<1
, then there are
\tfrac{\eta}{6^{d}}
-sparse families
\mathcal{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{3^{d}}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,3^{d}
, and for each cube
Q\in\mathcal{S}
there are
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,3^{d}\}
and
R\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
with
Q\subseteq R
and
|R|\leq 6^{d}|Q|
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}
is finite, then all families
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,3^{d}
can be taken to be finite.

3.
Averaging operators

As before, throughout this section we let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. Moreover, we fix measurable subsets
E,E^{\prime}
of
\Omega
with
0<\mu(E),\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
. Then, we define the averaging operator
T_{E,E^{\prime}}
by

T_{E,E^{\prime}}(\vec{f}):=\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\ind_{E^{\prime}}

acting on
m
-tuples of functions
\vec{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
with
f_{j}\ind_{E}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
. If
E=E^{\prime}
, then we set
T_{E}:=T_{E,E}
.

We wish to obtain a characterization of the boundedness of
T_{E,E^{\prime}}
from

\vec{\mathbf{X}}:=\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathbf{X}_{m},

where each
\mathbf{X}_{j}
is a
\mathcal{H}_{j}
-directional Banach space, into an
\mathcal{H}
-directional quasi-Banach space
\mathbf{Y}
in terms of reducing operators.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{j}
be a
\mathcal{H}_{j}
-directional Banach function space over
\Omega
with the component-wise saturation property for each
j=1,\ldots,m
, and let
\mathbf{Y}
be a
\mathcal{H}
-directional quasi-Banach function space over
\Omega
with the Fatou property. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

f_{j}\in\mathbf{X}_{j}
is integrable over
E
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
, and
T_{E,E^{\prime}}:\vec{\mathbf{X}}\to\mathbf{Y}
;

(2)

\ind_{E}v\in\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime}
for all
v\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
, and
\ind_{E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{Y}
for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}
.

In this case, we have

(3.1)

\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}\to\mathbf{Y}}\eqsim_{\vec{n},K_{\mathbf{Y}}}\mu(E)^{-m}\Big\|A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime},E}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}.

We note that since
A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}
and
A_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime},E}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
are positive definite and thus Hermitian operators, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime},E}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}=\Big\|\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}A_{\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime},E}\Big)A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}.

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we need to review some elementary tensor algebra.

Fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and define

\vec{\mathcal{H}}_{(i)}:=(\mathcal{H}_{i},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{m}),\quad\vec{\mathcal{H}}^{(i)}:=(\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{i})

as well as

\mathcal{H}_{(i)}:=\bigotimes_{j=i}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{j},\quad\mathcal{H}^{(i)}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{H}_{j}.

We also set
\mathcal{H}^{(0)}=\mathcal{H}_{(m+1)}:=\mathbf{F}
. Given now
\vec{u}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
, we define

\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)\Big(\bigotimes_{j=i}^{m}v_{j}\Big):=\Big(\prod_{j=i}^{m}\langle u_{j},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\Big)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i-1}u_{j}\in\mathcal{H}^{(i-1)},\quad\forall\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}_{(i)}

as well as

\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}v_{j}\Big):=\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{i}\langle u_{j},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\Big)\bigotimes_{j=i+1}^{m}u_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{(i+1)},\quad\forall\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}^{(i)}.

These definitions arise from the canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{H}_{j}\ni u_{j}\mapsto\langle u_{j},\cdot\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{j}^{*},\quad j=1,\ldots,m,

where
\mathcal{H}_{j}^{*}
denotes the space of sesquilinear forms on
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, and noticing then that these yield canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{H}\cong\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{H}_{j}\Big)\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=i}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{j}^{*}\Big)

and

\mathcal{H}\cong\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{H}_{j}^{*}\Big)\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=i+1}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{j}\Big).

By linearity, we can then consider the products
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{w}
, for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{(i)}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{H}^{(i)}
. One can verify through a direct computation that

(3.2)

\Big\langle\mathbf{u}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=i}^{m}v_{j}\Big),\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{(i-1)}}=\Big\langle\mathbf{u},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and

(3.3)

\Big\langle\mathbf{u}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}v_{j}\Big),\bigotimes_{j=i+1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{(i+1)}}=\Big\langle\mathbf{u},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
.

Finally, we note the identity

(3.4)

(\ldots(((\mathbf{u}v_{1})v_{2})v_{3})\ldots)v_{m}=\Big\langle\mathbf{u},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Assume first that all
f_{j}\in\mathbf{X}_{j}
are integrable over
E
for
j=1,\ldots,m
and
T_{E}:\vec{\mathbf{X}}\to\mathbf{Y}
. To see that
\ind_{E}u\in\mathbf{X}
for all
u\in\mathcal{H}
, note that, by linearity, it suffices to show this for
\mathbf{u}=\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}
with
u_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
. As
\mathbf{X}_{j}
possesses the component-wise saturation property, by
[
Nie24b
, Proposition 3.4]
we have that the
\mathbf{F}
-Banach function space
(\mathbf{X}_{j})_{u_{j}}
is saturated, therefore by
[
LN24
, Proposition 2.5]
there exists a weak order unit
\rho_{j}\in(\mathbf{X}_{j})_{u_{j}}
with
\rho_{j}>0

\mu
-a.e. on
\Omega
, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Observe that
\rho_{j}u_{j}\in\mathbf{X}_{j}
, for all
j=1,\ldots,m
, and thus

\prod_{j=1}^{m}\langle\rho_{j}\rangle_{E}\ind_{E^{\prime}}u=T_{E,E^{\prime}}(\rho_{1}u_{1},\ldots,\rho_{m}u_{m})\in\mathbf{Y}.

Since
\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{m}\langle\rho_{j}\rangle_{E}>0
, we conclude that
\ind_{E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{Y}
, as asserted.

Next, let
\vec{v}\in\vec{\mathcal{H}}
be arbitrary. Then, for all
f_{j}\in\mathbf{X}_{j}
with
\|f_{j}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}=1
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, letting
u_{j}:=\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
we estimate

\displaystyle\mu(E)^{-m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Big|\int_{E}\langle f_{j}(x),v_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|=\prod_{j=1}^{m}|\langle\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}|=\Big|\Big\langle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j},\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|}{\Big\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\Big(\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathbf{Y}}}\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\|_{\mathbf{Y}}\leq\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}\to\mathbf{Y}}\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j},\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|}{\Big\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\Big(\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathbf{Y}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}\to\mathbf{Y}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\mathbf{u},\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|}{\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{Y}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}\to\mathbf{Y}}\Big\|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{Y}}},

where we observe that

\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{Y}}}<\infty

exactly as in the proof of part (a) in
[
Nie24b
, Proposition 4.1]
, or alternatively, as a consequence of (
2.5
). Therefore, for each
j=1,\ldots,m
, by fixing
f_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
with
i\neq j
and letting
f_{j}
vary, from (
2.1
) we deduce
\ind_{E}v_{j}\in\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\prime}
.

Next, we estimate

\displaystyle R:=\mu(E)^{m}\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}\|_{\vec{\mathbf{X}}\to\mathbf{Y}}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\mu(E)^{m}\|T_{E,E^{\prime}}(\vec{f})\|_{\mathbf{Y}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\mu(E)^{m}\Big\|\ind_{E^{\prime}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathbf{Y}}\overset{\eqref{eq:reducing_matrix}}{\eqsim_{K_{\mathbf{Y}},\vec{n}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\mu(E)^{m}\Big\|A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\mu(E)^{m}\Big|\Big\langle A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big),\mathbf{u}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\mu(E)^{m}\Big|\Big\langle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E},A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|.

Let us fix for the moment
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{H}
as well as
f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}
for
j=2,\ldots,m
and estimate

R_{1}:=\mu(E)\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E},A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big|.

Thanks to (
3.2
) we can write

\displaystyle R_{1}=\mu(E)\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\langle f_{1}\rangle_{E},(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})\Big(\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big|.

Setting

u_{1}:=(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})\Big(\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\in\mathcal{H}_{1},

we can continue the computation using (
2.1
) to find that

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu(E)\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\langle f_{1}\rangle_{E},u_{1}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big|=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Big|\int_{E}\langle f_{1}(x),u_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|

\displaystyle=\|\ind_{E}u_{1}\|_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime}}\eqsim_{n_{1}}\|A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\eqsim_{\vec{n}}\mu(E)^{m-1}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=2,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\Big\|A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}\Big[(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})\Big(\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mu(E)^{m-1}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=2,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\sup_{v_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}\Big|\Big\langle A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}\Big[(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})\Big(\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big],v_{1}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\mu(E)^{m-1}\sup_{v_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=2,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})\Big(\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big),A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}v_{1}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:partial_tensor_inner_upper},\eqref{eq:partial_tensor_inner_lower}}{=}\mu(E)^{m-1}\sup_{v_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=2,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\bigotimes_{j=2}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E},(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})(A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}v_{1})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{(2)}}\Big|.

Then, similarly to previously we deduce

\displaystyle R\eqsim_{\vec{n}}\mu(E)^{m-2}\sup_{v_{1}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathbf{X}_{j}}\\
j=3,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\Big\|A_{\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\prime},E}\Big[\Big[(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})(A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}v_{1})\Big]\Big(\bigotimes_{j=3}^{m}\langle f_{j}\rangle_{E}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}.

Continuing inductively, we eventually arrive at

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\eqsim_{\vec{n}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\\
j=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}}}\Big|[\ldots[[(A_{\mathbf{Y},E^{\prime}}\mathbf{u})A_{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\prime},E}v_{1}](A_{\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\prime},E}v_{2})]\ldots](A_{\mathbf{X}_{m}^{\prime},E}v_{m})]\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j}\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\\
